MR. CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
TRIBUTES TO MARTRYS SARDAR BHAGAT SINGH, RAJGURU AND SUKHDEV 
MR. CHAIRMAN:   Hon.  Members, today, the 23rd of March, marks the anniversary 
of the martyrdom of three of the most valia nt sons of Mother India — Sardar Bhagat 
Singhji, Rajguruji and Sukhdevji — who laid down their lives on this day in 1931 in the 
prime of their youth. Their unparalleled courage, indomitable spirit, and supreme 
sacrifice remain a shining ch apter in our recent struggle for freedom and continue to 
evoke deep respect and gratitude from every citizen of India. 
Hon. Members, their martyrdom was not merely an episode in the history of 
our Freedom Movement, it was a defining moment that awakened the conscience of 
the nation and inspired millions to rise in unity against colonial rule. The freedom that 
we cherish today is the result of the sacr ifice of countless patrio ts and, among them, 
this heroic trio occupies a special place in the heart of every Indian. 
On this solemn occasion, let us reaffirm our resolve to uphold the constitutional 
values and democratic principles for whic h these martyrs stood and to work unitedly 
for a strong, inclusive and progressive India. 
I request the hon. Members to rise in  their places and observe silence as a 
mark of respect to the immortal memory of Sardar Bhagat Singh ji, Rajguru ji and 
Sukhdevji. 
(Hon. Members then stood in silence for one minute.) 
MR. CHAIRMAN:   Now, Papers to be laid on the Table.

Significant milestone in the democratic history of India 
SHRI DORJEE TSHERING LEPCHA (Sikkim) : Hon. Chairman,  Sir, with immense 
honour that I stand before this august House today to highlight a defining moment in 
India’s political history – one that celebra tes the unparalleled leadership of Prime

nation’s history, as he has surpassed the record once held by Shri Pawan Chamling, 
who served for 8,930 days as Chief Minister of Sikkim. ...(Interruptions)...

the marginalized.

Need to promote public awareness of 'The Light and the Lotus' exhibition                   
in New Delhi 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated) : Sir, today, I stand here to talk about the 
"The Light and the Lotus: Relics of th e Awakened One" about Bhagwan Buddha. 
The Exhibition is held in Qila Rai Pithora.  It is not an ordinary Exhibition. Please 
understand the significance of that. When Buddha died 2,500 years ago, his bones 
and Chita Bhasma were distributed into eight different parts, and one part was given 
to Shakya Kula. It is his clan; and the Stup a was built in Piprahwa; and that is one of 
the most sacred ones.  
Sir, 127 years back, that is, in 1898, it was excavated and part of that was 
taken by Mr.  William Peppe to Britain, and he kept th e jewels along with many other 
things.  Now, in 1971, they really excavated it, and they got his bones and some more 
jewels. It is not the jewels or bones. If you go and see that Exhibition, you can see the 
artifacts which were 2,500 year s old -- extremely difficult to  make even today -- with 
the Brahmi script, at that time, indicating,  ‘We, the Shakya people,  would like to 
place a small ornament along with the Buddha's bones and it is a part of our 
contribution to Buddha's glory.’ It is one of  the finest Exhibitions which one can ever 
see.  It is not just a bone. Buddha was a near perfection man, and he is internationally 
known today. But he belongs to everyone, and we should be very proud that he was 
born in our border. It was not easy to br ing those jewels which were taken by Mr. 
William Peppe to Britain. Bringing back those jewels along with those containers was 
next to impossible. It was not possible at all in one's lifetime. But I would like to 
congratulate the following people: Number one is, our Prime Minister, Shri Narendra 
Modi. In his stewardship, it was brought within four months of time, and he saw to it 
that it should come to India. The next pers on in this line is, Shri Gajendra Shekhawat, 
who worked very hard because these were out of our border and were in Hong Kong. 
Bringing them was not easy. Number three is, Shri Vivek Aggarwal, Secretary, and 
his team. At number four are many donors , many people who took risk because 
bringing these things would be very difficu lt one. So, at last, we have Buddha's 
bones, a near perfection man. I will end this with:

Concerns over India’s participation in Pax Silica and its implications for data privacy 
SHRI DIGVIJAYA SINGH (Madhya Pradesh) :  S i r ,  w i t h  y o u r  k i n d  p e r m i s s i o n ,  I  a m  
going to take more than three minutes. I ri se to draw the attention of this august 
House to an important and emerging issue, that is, significance and implications of 
Pax Silica for India’s strategic autonomy, data  privacy and technological sovereignty.  
India has recently joined an international technology coalition known as Pax Silica.   In 
simple terms, Pax Silica is a grouping of co untries aimed at co-operation in standard 
setting in critical areas such as semiconductors, artificial intelligence and digital 
infrastructure, with the dominant role played by the United States.  
 While this initiative may offer certain benefits such as access to secure supply 
chains, increased investments and advance technologies, it also raises serious 
concerns that merit urgent discussion.  The foremost concern relates to data privacy 
and sovereignty.  If India is required to  align and share its standards and systems 
under this coalition, there is a possibilit y that Indian data may be persisted or 
governed through foreign platforms or frameworks.  This raises critical questions 
about the effectiveness and the enforceability of India’s Digital Personal Data 
Protection Act, 2023.  Would the private data of each Indian citizen be protected 
under the DPDP Act?  We want to know th is from the Government.  Secondly, there 
are apprehensions that India’s long-term policy autonomy in such areas as AI 
development, semiconductor strategy and di gital infrastructure may be constrained 
by external standards and over sight.  This could potentially dilute our long-standing 
principle of strategic autonomy and impact  the Government’s stated ‘India First’ 
approach.  In view of these concerns, I urge the Governme nt to clarify (i) the terms 
and conditions of India’s participation in Pax Silica, (ii) the safeguards in place to 
ensure protection and sovereignty of Indian citizens’ data, and (iii) clarify whether this 
arrangement affects India’s inde pendent policy making in criti cal technology sectors.   
This is not merely a technological matter, but one that concerns national security, 
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
19

 
economic sovereignty and the rights of our citizens.  The Government must place all 
relevant facts before the House with full transparency.  Sir, I will take little more time.  
MR. CHAIRMAN:  Please go ahead.  
SHRI DIGVIJAYA SINGH :  Thank you, Sir.  Rahul Gandhiji has rightly compared the 
value of data, the backbone of 21 st century AI era, to crude oil.  By signing Pax Silica 
unconditionally, is it a step towards digital colonialism?   This is something which 
concerns every citizen of this country, and also his privacy.  Sir, I am concluding as 
per your time limit, but next time, given a chance - oh, sorry, I am retiring now.   
MR. CHAIRMAN : I am giving you time now because after this Session, we will be 
missing you and your very good experience in political life.  Please conclude.

Concern over the growing menace of VIP culture and inconvenience to the           
public in the country 
SHRIMATI JAYA AMITABH BACHCHAN (Uttar Pradesh) :  Sir, I am very glad that 
today you have given protection to Lepcha ji.  We are hoping the same for the rest of 
us, especially the other smaller parties.  I will include this attitude to my subject of ‘VIP 
culture’ that is very prevalent today.  
Sir, VIP culture has become a malaise in the society, especially in the national 
capital.  We have witnessed a growing phen omenon where movement of VIPs, be it 
politicians or high-ranking officials or dign itaries, leads to severe inconvenience for 
26
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
the common tax-paying people.  The roadbloc ks and traffic diversions, ostensibly for 
the safety and convenience of  these VIPs, have become a major source of disruption 
for daily lives of countless citizens.  This disruptive and often detrimental VIP culture 
prevails, especially in the national capital.  One has travelled all over the world but has 
never faced this.   One has seen many VIPs and cavalcades going, but they never 
stop people on the street.  The other da y, the MPs of this House who leave from 
Shardul Gate were stopped.  The main door was locked because there was a VIP 
movement.  I have never faced such humiliation in the last 22 years of mine in this 
Parliament.  We are not a threat to any of th e VIPs, especially, the political lot.  There 
are some roads where political bigwigs ar e living and they are blocked.  It is 
impossible for us to come to Parliament.  We have to wait for half an hour.  We have 
to time our departure from the House to atte nd Parliament on time.  So many times I 
have been delayed because of this.   Sir, on the farewell party, it took me one hour to 
reach there!  One hour because there was a VIP movement!  Every citizen was angry.  
I am telling you, Sir, if this is not corrected, there will be road rage.  It is so difficult 
when I am stuck in traffic.  Ambulances are being stopped. …(time-bell)… Sir, you are 
in a very generous mood today.  
MR. CHAIRMAN:  You have to conclude, Madam.   
SHRIMATI JAYA AMITABH BACHCHAN : We need your protection. We are Members 
of this House of Parliament and cannot be  humiliated the way we were the other day. 
Sir, please speak to the Government – you are the Vice-President of this country – 
that this culture has to stop in order to give dignity to the taxpayers of this country 
who have brought us into this House today. Thank you very much.

Concern over exorbitant school fees imposing financial burden on common families 
SHRIMATI RAJANI ASHOKRAO PATIL (Maharashtra) : Hon. Chairman, Sir, I rise 
today to raise a matter of urgent public importance -- the uncontrolled and ever-
increasing fee structure in private sch ools across our country, which is putting 
unbearable pressure on lakhs of middle-class and lower-middle-class families.  
Sir, education is not a luxury; it is a fundamental right. Yet, today, for many 
parents, educating their children in so-ca lled ‘good schools’ has become a financial 
burden, bordering on distress. Every year, we witness arbitrary fee hikes by private 
schools, often without any transparency or justification. Along with tuition fees, 
parents are compelled to pay various addition al charges such as ‘development fees’, 
‘activity charges’, ‘smart class fees’, and are even forced to purchase books and 
uniforms from selected vendors at inflated prices.  
Sir, this reflects the growing commercia lization of education, which goes 
against the very spirit of our Constitution. I would like to remind this august House 
that under the visionary leadership of UPA Government, the landmark Right to 
Education Act, 2009, was enacted. It ensure d that 25 per cent of seats in private 
schools are reserved for children from Econom ically Weaker Sections, providing them 
free and compulsory education. This wa s a historic step towards inclusive and 
equitable education. However, today, we are witnessing a situation where, on the 
one hand, that spirit of inclusivity is weak ening and, on the other hand, the remaining 
75 per cent of parents are being subjected to unchecked financial burden.   Sir, while 
some States have enacted fee regulation laws, their implementation remains weak 
and inconsistent. There is an urgent n eed for a strong, transparent and uniform 
regulatory mechanism to monitor and control school fees across the country.  
MR. CHAIRMAN : Digvijaya Singhji, please pay yo ur attention to the Member’s 
submission.    
SHRIMATI RAJANI ASHOKRAO PATIL: Sir, I urge the Government to ensure strict 
regulation of fee hikes; mandate full transparency in school finan ces; curb hidden and 
compulsory charges; protect the rights and in terests of parents and students.  If we 
fail to act now, quality edu cation will become the privilege  of a few rather than the 
right of every child. Education must empower; it should not impoverish families. 
Thank you.

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Electric buses to strengthen urban public transport in major cities of Bihar 
#*316.    DR. BHIM SINGH: Will the Minister  of Housing and Urban Affairs be pleased 
to state: 
(a)  whether any plan has been approv ed or proposed for operating electric 
buses (EV buses) to strengthen urban public transport in major cities of the 
State of Bihar on the lines of Delhi, if so, the details thereof; and 
 (b) the citiesin Biharthat havebeen incl udedor areproposed tobe included under 
the said plan along with the details of  number of EV buses, financial support 
and the expected time lines thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF HOUSING AND U RBAN AFFAIRS (SHRI MANOHAR LAL) : (a) to 
(b) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b)  Urban Transport is a part of Urban planning which is a State 
subject.  Therefore, respective State Governments are responsible for planning, 
implementation and management of urban transport system including strengthening 
of urban public transport in their respective cities.  
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
34
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
However, under PM-eBus Sewa scheme, 10,000 air-conditioned e-buses have 
been sanctioned for 116 cities across 26 States/UTs on Public Private Partnership 
model, subject to participation in PM -eBus Sewa Payment Security Mechanism 
(PSM) Scheme.  In addition, Central Fina ncial Assistance is also provided for 
creation of Civil Depot and Behind-the-Met er (BTM) Power Infrastructure for buses 
covered under the Scheme. This Scheme also envisages to develop a Central 
Intelligent Transit Management System (ITMS) platform to help cities in monitoring 
daily operation of the buses deployed under the Scheme. 
 Under this scheme, a total of 400 e-buse s has been sanctioned for 6 cities of 
Bihar namely Bhagalpur, Darbhanga, Gaya, Muzaffarpur, Patna, Purnea and, so far, 
a total of ₹ 112.46 Cr has been sanctioned for development of Associated 
Infrastructure i.e. Behind-the-Meter (BTM) Power infrastructure and Civil Depot 
Infrastructure. The city-wise details of buses sanctioned, amount sanctioned and 
released are provided in Annexure.  Delivery and operation of  e-Buses are dependent 
on completion of civil depot and BTM Power infrastructure which is the responsibility 
of the concerned State Government.  
Apart from above, under a sub-scheme head namely “Transport Planning and 
Capacity Building in Urban Transpor t for metro and non-metro projects ”, 100% 
Central Financial Assistance (CFA) for und ertaking the activities relating to 
preparation of Comprehensive Mobility Plan (CMP)/Detailed Project Report 
(DPR)/Research studies etc. is provided to  the cities for strengthening their public 
transport system.

buses in Patna City. All these 25 e-buses have been deployed.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Now, Q. No.317. 
Urban flooding and water conservation strategies 
*317. SHRI UJJWAL DEORAO NIKAM : Will the Minister of HOUSING AND URBAN 
AFFAIRS be pleased to state: 
(a)  the specific steps taken by Governme nt to reduce or prevent the recurring 
issues ofurban flooding and water logging in major cities; 
(b)  the manner in which Government plans to conserve and augment 
groundwaterresources through its existing urban development schemes; 
(c)  whether Government has formulated a long-term plan to address the 
discharge of untreated sewage into water bodies; 
(d)  if so, the details thereof ; and 
(e)  the manner in which water quality assessment is being conducted under the 
AMRUT mission across various States, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF HOUSING AND U RBAN AFFAIRS (SHRI MANOHAR LAL) : (a) to 
(e) A Statement is laid in the Table of the House.  
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
41

 
Statement 
(a)   Water and Sanitation is State su bject and Management of urban flooding 
falls under the purview of the State Go vernments and the Urban Local Bodies 
(ULBs)/Urban Development Authorities, who are responsible for maintaining the 
drainage and sewerage system. Government of India supplements the efforts of the 
States through schematic interventions/ advisories. It provides financial and technical 
support to the States to strengthen Urban Planning ecosystem.  
Usually, increased incidence of high intensity rainfall in short duration is mainly 
responsible for urban floods, which is fu rther compounded by unplanned growth, 
encroachment of natural water bodies, inadequate sewer systems, inadequate 
rainwater drainage systems, encroachments, etc.   
Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (A MRUT) launched in 
the year 2015, inter alia, has a component on storm water drainage, which involved 
construction and improvement of drains/ st orm water drains in order to reduce and 
eliminate flooding and creating green space s and parks. Under AMRUT, 838 Storm 
Water Drainage projects worth ₹3017.13 crore have been taken up and 3,798 water 
logging points have been eliminated so far. 
Further, 2,522 green spaces and park projects worth ₹1,604.13 crore have 
been taken up by the States/UTs under AMRUT and 5,286 acres of permeable green 
space area have been developed so far through these projects. 
Under AMRUT 2.0, projects for green sp aces and parks and rejuvenation of 
water bodies are taken up by the States. Under AMRUT 2.0, proposals of the States/ 
UTs for 2,991 water body rejuvenation projects worth ₹6,083.32 crorecovering around 
1,21,800 acres of area and 1,665 gr een spaces and park projects worth ₹1,103.71 
crore covering 13,893.82 acre, have been approved.  
Besides, under AMRUT and AMRUT 2.0 sewerage and septage management 
projects have been undertaken, which inter alia help in evacuation of storm water. 
Under AMRUT, as updated by State/UTs on AMRUT portal, 889 sewerage/septage 
management projects have been grounded, which has led to crea tion of 22,477 km 
length of sewer network. Under AMRUT 2.0, the State/UTs have taken up 584 
Sewerage and Septage Management projects covering 34,559 KM of sewer network. 
42
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) has published the 
following documents/advisory guidelines for improving urban drainage and flood 
management: 
i. Urban and Regional Development Pl ans Formulation and Implementation 
(URDPFI) Guidelines, 2014  
(https://mohua.gov.in/upload/uploadfiles/files/URDPFI%20Guidelines%20 
Vol% 20I(2).pdf ) 
ii. Standard Operating Proced ure (SOP) for Urban Flooding 
(https://mohua.gov.in/upload/uploadfiles/files/SOP%20Urban%20flooding
_5%20May%202017.pdf) 
iii. River Centric Urban Planning Guidelin es in 2021 to enable cities in the 
development of conjunctive water mana gement approaches including nature-
based solution 
(https://mohua.gov.in/upload/uploadfiles/files/RCUP%20Guidelines.pdf) 
iv. Guidance Document on Creation of Rain Water Harvesting 
Parks(https://mohua.gov.in/pdf/6566e1048ab41guidance-document-on-
rainwater-harvesting-parks-final.pdf) 
v.  Manual on Storm Wa ter Drainage System 
(https://mohua.gov.in/publication/manual-on-storm-water-drainage-
systems--2019.php)  
 
The Government has launched the Urba n Flood Risk Management Programme 
(UFRMP) under the National Disaster Mitigation Fund (NDMF) in 2021. This initiative 
specifically targets seven major metropo litan cities namely, Mumbai, Chennai, 
Kolkata, Bengaluru, Hyderabad, Ahmedaba d, and Pune, with a total allocation of 
₹2,500 crore. The UFRMP Phase II, with a budget of ₹2,200 crores, targets flood risk 
mitigation in eleven cities (Tier-II ci ties) namely, Guwahati, Patna, Kanpur, 
Trivandrum, Visakhapatnam, Bhubaneswar,  Bhopal, Jaipur, Indore, Lucknow, and 
Raipur with an allocation of Rs. 222.22 crore each[INR 200 Cr from NDMF(90%) and 
INR 22.22 Cr from State share (10%)] to  assess flood risks, develop preparedness 
plans, establish multi-hazard frameworks, and enhance community resilience. 
(b) AMRUT/AMRUT 2.0 promotes source sustainability, aquifer recharge, 
and scientific groundwater management. AMRUT 2.0advocates preparation of Aquifer 
Management Plan by the States/ UTs, which focuses on maintaining positive 
groundwater balance in urban aquifer systems.  
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
43

 
Rejuvenation of water bodies to augm ent water sources is one of the main 
components of AMRUT 2.0. The admi ssible elements under this include 
strengthening/ rejuvenation of aquifers / community wells, harvesting the rainwater 
through storm water drains into water body (which is not receiving sewage/ effluent), 
etc. Under AMRUT 2.0, 2,991 water body rejuvenation projects worth ₹6,083.32 
crorecovering around 1,21,800 acres of area have been approved. 
490 water supply schemes in AMRUT citi es are drawing 6,700 MLD of surface 
water under AMRUT thereby preventing groundwater extraction. 
Shallow Aquifer Management (SAM) initiative under AMRUT 2.0 was launched 
as a pilot project across 9 diverse Indian cities. The initiative aimed to demonstrate 
the effectiveness of strategic interventions in managing shallow aquifers, focusing on 
aquifer mapping, construction and restoratio n of recharge structures, and integration 
of groundwater management into urban planning frameworks. Under SAM 2.0, 75 
additional cities have been covered.  
Under AMRUT 2.0 Reforms, Municipal Corporation and Urban Local Bodies 
are incentivized for creating artificial recharge and storage structures under the Jal 
Sanchay Jan Bhagidari (JSJB) initiative la unched under National Water Mission's Jal 
Shakti Abhiyan: Catch the Rain. Under this in itiative, there is provision for incentive of 
₹2 crore each for the top 10  Municipal Corporations, ₹1 crore each for the next 10 
Municipal Corporations and ₹0.40 crore each for the 50 UL Bs (other than Municipal 
Corporations). The incentive amount can be utilized  by Municipal Corporation/ ULBs 
for water conservation structures and wa ter conservation awareness activities, 
including capacity building of stakeholders. 
(c) and (d) AMRUT 2.0 has been envisa ged to promote circular economy of 
water through development of city water ba lance plan for each city focusing on 
recycle/ reuse of treated sewage, reju venation of water bodies and water 
conservation.  
Under AMRUT, States/ UTs have ta ken up 889 sewerage / septage 
management projects worth ₹34,471.1 crore. A total of 5,178 MLD of STP capacity 
has been created under AMRUT projects of which 1,437 MLD capacity has been 
developed for recycle/reuse. Under AMRU T 2.0, the State Water Action Plans 
(SWAPs) comprising of 584 sewerage/ se ptage management projects with a total 
cost of ₹65,624.98 crore have been  approved so far,covering 6,649 MLD sewage 
treatment plant capacity of which around 1932 MLD is for recycle/reuse. 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
Through AMRUT/ AMRUT 2.0 and in conv ergence with the States, 182 lakh 
sewer connections (including households covered through Fecal Sludge and Septage 
Management) have been provided in AMRUT Cities, thus mitigating the sewerage 
flow into the water bodies. 
Further, "Jal Hi AMRIT" initiative has been launched under AMRUT 2.0 
reforms, which aims to incentivize States and Union Territories (UTs) to efficiently 
manage sewage treatment plants for recycl able treated water meeting environmental 
standards on sustained basis. The focus of this initiative is building capacity and 
incentivizing qualitative improvements in the treated discharge effluent. To 
institutionalize circularity, Water Reso urce Recovery Cells (WRRCs) have been 
established in 25 States/ Union Territories und er this initiative to plan, monitor, and 
scale resource recovery interventions. 
Swachh Bharat Mission – Urban (SBM-U) 2.0 launched on 1st, October, 
2021 includes a new component ‘Used Water management (UWM)’. Under UWM 
component, financial assistance is provided  to the Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) with 
population less than 1 Lakh for setting up of STPs/ STP-cum-FSTP and laying 
Interception and Diversion (IandD) structures including provision of pumping stations 
and pumping main/gravity main upto STP, to ensure that all used water is safely 
collected, treated and reused to feasible  extent. Under SBM, 10,877 MLD sewerage 
treatment capacity has been approved.  
(e) Management and maintaining the qua lity of water as per the applicable 
standards in urban areas is also the resp onsibility of State Government/ Urban Local 
Bodies (ULBs).  
To ensure safe drinking water supply to the urban households, 
AMRUT/AMRUT 2.0 supports, rehabilitation and upgradation of water supply network 
and creation/augmentation of Water Treatment Plants (WTPs) plants. Under 
AMRUT/ AMRUT2.0, a total of 93,457.51 km of water pipeline network has been laid/ 
replaced. So far, 6140 Million Litre pe r Day (MLD) of WTP capacity has been 
approved under AMRUT, of which, 5,42 9 MLD WTP capacity has been created. 
Under AMRUT 2.0, 11,395 MLD WTP capacity has been approved so far. These 
treatment plants are generally equipped with inhouse lab fac ilities or have tie up with 
the Labs for regular water quality testing. 
Further, AMRUT 2.0 Mission promotes  24x7 water supply with quality 
assurance, Drink from Tap (DfT) quality wa ter in selected District Metered Areas 
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
45

 
(DMAs)/wards, online water quality monitoring, sensors and Supervisory Control and 
Data Acquisition (SCADA), community part icipation, especially Women Self Help 
Groups (SHGs), in water quality testing.  
407 projects with 1,153 DMAs benefitting  16.72 lakh Households have been 
approved in 348 ULBs under AMRUT 2. 0.258 water supply schemes have smart 
monitoring systems such as SCADA system under AMRUT, an d 1,422 water supply 
projects under AMRUT 2.0 has provision for SCADA system.  
Under AMRUT Mitra initiative, women Self  Help Groups are engaged for water 
quality testing in the field by the ULBs. 
Drinking water quality arrangement in urban areas is gover ned by standards 
and guidelines issued by the Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) primarily IS 10500: 
Drinking Water - Specification. The Mini stry has published Manual on Water Supply 
and Treatment Systems (Drink  from Tap) in March 2024 
(https://mohua.gov.in/publication/manual-on-water-supply-and-treatment-
systems---drink-from-tap---march-2024.php), for reference by the States/ ULBs 
for designing and implementation of the wa ter supply projects. Details of the sanitary 
survey and the methodology to carry out wate r quality testing have been explained in 
Section 7.9 of Chapter 7: “Water Quality Testing and Laboratory Facilities” in Part A of 
this manual. 
The Ministry has also issued an advi sory in November, 2024 to formulate a 
Water Quality Monitoring strategy at the State or City level. The advisory 
"Strengthening Water Quality Monitoring in  Cities through Community Participation" 
(https://amrut.mohua.gov.in/assets/Water-Quality-Monitoring-WQMIS-Advisory-
Final.pdf) aims to strengthen the institutiona l capacity for citywide monitoring of 
drinking water quality in urban areas along with Digital Display Boards for water quality 
parameters for public information and awareness. 
ULBs/ Parastatal conduct testing of water at Water Treatment Plants (WTPs) 
and household levels as per applicable norm s. As reported by the States/UTs on the 
AMRUT 2.0 platform during the period from 1st January, 2024 to 31st December, 
2024, a total of 3,32,170 water quality samp les out of 3,35,278 samples tested at 
WTPs and 22,18,838 samples out of 22,45,2 00 samples tested at the household level 
were found compliant for the parameters E. coli, Arsenic and Fluoride.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Q. No. 318. 
Water crisis in the coal sector 
318.  SHRIMATI SAGARIKA GHOSE : Will the Minister of POWER be pleased to 
state: 
(a)  units of coal-generated power lost due to water shortages since 2023, year-
wise; 
(b)  the details of power plant shut down due to water shortages since 2023; 
(c)  the details of new projects shortliste d for future operations that are located 
in area sclassified as either suffering from water scarcity or stress; 
(d)  whether there have been farmer pr otests and local diversions over the 
effects of watershortage; and 
(e)  compensation to affected districts for economic losses from halted output? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINI STRY OF POWER (SHRI SHRIPAD YESSO 
NAIK):  (a) to (e) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b)  As per details submitted by the generating utilities, there has 
been no shutdown of coal based power pl ants due to water shortage. However, the 
coal-based plants have intimated some ge neration loss due to other water related 
issues (pipeline leakages, desalination plan t outage etc.) and the year-wise details 
since 2023 are given at Annexure-I. 
(c)  As per the National Compilation on  Dynamic Ground Water Resources 
of India, 2025 of the Ministry of Jal Shakti , groundwater assessment units ( districts, 
blocks etc) across the country are categorised as Safe (less or equal to 70%), 
Semi-critical (greater than 70% and less or equal to 90%), Critical (greater than 
90% and less or equal to 100%) and Over Exploited ( greater than 100%)  based 
on ratio of annual groundwater extraction and replenishment of  phreatic aquifer. 
Accordingly, there are three (03) numbers ofcoal-based power projects under 
construction or expansion located in water- stressed districts and their details are at 
Annexure-II. 
[ 23 March, 2026 ]

Generation being a de-licensed activity any decision related to establishment/ 
operationalization of coal-based thermal power plant is taken by the generating 
company/project developer based on their techno-economic and commercial 
requirements and assessment of local conditions, which includes availability of water. 
(d) and (e) Ministry of Power has not received any report regarding farmer 
protests on account of diversion/scarcity of water.

Coal exchange and mine closure reforms 
*319 SHRI ARUN SINGH: Will the Minister of COAL be pleased to state: 
(a)   the current status of the Coal Trading Exchange (CTE); 
(b)  out of the 131 coal blocks allocated under the Commercial Coal Mining Policy 
the  numbers that have commenced actual production as of February, 2026; 
(c)  the penalties that have been imposed for delays in achieving the milestones; 
(d) whether Government has completed the revised Jharia Master Plan for 
rehabilitation and the new technologies which are being deployed to manage 
long standing underground mine fires; and 
(e)   if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF COAL AND MINES (SHRI G. KISHAN REDDY):  (a) to (e) A 
Statement is laid on the table of the House. 
Statement 
  (a)  Ministry of Coal has initiated the pro cess of setting up of Coal Exchange 
(s) in the country in pursuance of the legisl ative provisions of the Mines and Minerals 
(Development and Regulation) Amendment Act, 2025. Section 18B of the Mines and 
Minerals (Development and Regulation) Amendment Act, 2025 empowers the Central 
Government to frame the rules for the purpose of Coal Exchange (s). In pursuance of 
the provisions, Ministry of Coal has initiate d the process of formulation of the Coal 
Exchange Rules, which shall provide for the objectives and structure of the Coal 
Exchange (s) to be established in the country. 
(b) Out of the 131 coal blocks allocated  under the Commercial Coal Mining 
Policy, 21 coal blocks have obtained Mine  Opening Permission and 12 coal blocks 
have commenced production as on 28th February, 2026. 
(c) The details of penalties imposed ag ainst coal blocks allocated under 
commercial
 auction scheme post 2020 is at the Annexure.  
(d) and (e) Government has approved the Revised Jharia Master Plan (JMP) 
in June, 2025 for dealing with Fire, Subsidence and Rehabilitation of affected families 
58
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
of Jharia Coalfield. The initial phase of Revised JMP is applicable for the 81 most 
vulnerable sites and the initial phase is to be completed by December, 2028.   
The fire is being extinguished by diggi ng out through excavation or blanketing 
by overburden (OB). To monitor the success of this process, Bharat Coking Coal 
Limited periodically conducts surveys thro ugh the National Remote Sensing Centre 
(NRSC), Hyderabad, for delineation of surface fires. 
As per the NRSC survey report of the year 2017-18, the surface fire area was 
3.26 sq. km, which has reduced to 1.53 sq. km in the year 2024-25. Further, as per 
the survey of the year 2020-21, there were 27 fire locations, out of which 17 have been 
dealt with by excavation and blanketing through OB as of now.

Minister is the world's most gifted writer in fiction.  It is because, if you look at the 
Budget, and then if you look at this Appropriation Bill that has been moved, it is very 
clear that the Budget presented to Parlia ment every year is a work of fiction.  I will tell 
you why!  
There is the Budget Estimates, which is presented in th e Budget, and then, 
after that, the Government comes out with  Revised Estimates. In 2025-2026, the 
Government has cut down its own Budget Esti mates by nearly Rs. 10 lakh crores in 
the Revised Estimates.  Rs. 10 lakh crores is the di fference between what they 
presented in the Budget versus what is in the Revised Esti mates.  This is nothing 
short of presenting a very glorious work of  fiction while presenting the Budget where 
everything is hunky-dory, we are going to spend so much and all, but the reality is 
something very concerning.

the Jal Jeevan Mission, -- water literally has been one of the basic necessities for a 
human to survive -- there is  a 69 per cent cut from the Budget Estimates to the 
Revised Estimates.  With regard to Labour  and Employment, this Government says, 
there is no unemployment. In Labour and Employment, there is 61 per cent cut from 
the Budget Estimates.  In Housing and Urban Affairs, how much was cut from the 
Budget Estimates to the Revised Estimates? It  is 38 per cent.  In Tourism, it is 50 per

SHRI S. KALYANASUNDARAM (Tamil Nadu):  & Hon. Madam Vice Chairman of this 
august House, leaders of all political parties, esteemed Ministers, and fellow 
Members of the House, greetings to one and a ll. I am not here to engage in political 
rhetoric;   rather,   I wish to speak abou t the essential needs of the people. I request 
you to grant me more time and attention.  "Perform every possible act of virtue, 
ceaselessly, wherever your path may lead."  According to the interpretation provided 
by Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi, this couplet of Tirukkural signifies that whatever actions 
one undertakes, they must be execu ted everywhere—without exception—in 
accordance with the path of righteousness. 
I wish to express my heartfelt gratitude to the hon. Chief Minister and hon. 
Deputy Chief Minister of Tamil Nadu, who have honoured me by granting me the 
opportunity to speak in this very Ho use where towering figures such as Perarignar 
Anna, Murasoli Maran, and Kanimozhi Ka runanidhi have prev iously delivered 
speeches representing Tamil Nadu on be half of the Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam 
(DMK). I also extend my sincere thanks  to you, Madam Vice Chairperson, for 
granting me the permission to address this House.  I also wish to convey my sincere 
gratitude to the Union Government for accepti ng my request, granting permission for 
the expansion of the Kumbakonam Railway  Station, and for relocating the "Goods 
Shed" facility from Kumbakonam to Thirunageswaram. 
Regarding the "100-Day Work Scheme "—specifically the Mahatma Gandhi 
National Rural Employment Guaran tee Scheme (MGNREGS)—the Union 
Government had previously borne the entire financial burden of this program. I wish to 
point out that not only has this scheme been renamed—thereby removing the name 
of the Father of the Nation, Mahatma Gandhi, and being rebranded as a "new 
scheme"—but new regulations have also  been introduced. These regulations 
mandate that State Governments must no w bear 40% of the scheme's funding 
costs, and the new norms established for the se lection of beneficiaries will, in my firm 
opinion, inevitably lead to the disruption and undermining of this vital program. 
Tamil Nadu is not merely a State; it st ands as a pioneer across a multitude of 
sectors, including educatio n, healthcare, industry, ag riculture, and renewable 
energy. Therefore, I earnestly request that adequate financial allocations be made to

provide the necessary impetus and support for the following initiatives. Of particular 
significance is the Mahamaham festival of 2028. Much like the Kumbh Mela held once

devotees from across the globe will visit for this festival. Therefore, measures must be 
taken to renovate the Thanjavur Airport and make it operational by 2027. 
Regarding the Kumbakonam  Railway Station, fund s have currently been 
allocated, and renovation work has al ready commenced. These works must be 
completed and the station made operational by  the end of this year; furthermore, to

Tourism. Taking into account the anticipated in flux of devotees visiting the city of

infrastructures, and comprehensive preparatory measures must be undertaken.

appropriate measures must be taken to es tablish covered storage facilities at the 
railway stations located in the Cauvery Delta region, specifically at Thanjavur, 
Kumbakonam, Thirunageswaram, Saliyaman galam, Tiruvarur, Mannargudi, and 
Needamangalam.

Taluk of Thanjavur District. This initiative would ensure that various districts—
including the Delta districts—do not experien ce issues related to low voltage. As this 
project is crucial for fostering new indust ries and preventing power shortages, I 
request that the necessary financial assistance be provided for its implementation.

Seaweed serves as a highly beneficial  resource for producing fertilizers 
essential for agriculture, particularly wi thin the framework of natural and organic 
farming methods. Given that Tamil Nadu possesses a climate highly conducive to 
large-scale seaweed production, I request that necessary training centers be 
established and that financial assistance, ac companied by subsidies, be provided to 
promote this sector. 
Madam, it is deeply distressing to learn that a sum of ₹3,528 crore has been 
withheld for Tamil Nadu on the ground th at the State has not implemented the three-
language formula as mandated by the New Education Policy. This is not merely a 
financial delay; rather, it appears to be a deliberate attempt to impede the educational 
advancement of Tamil Nadu. It is antithetical to the prin ciples of democracy when a 
State asserts its legitimate rights, yet face s delays and a lack of response from the 
Centre.  Despite the Chief Minister of Tam il Nadu having written a series of letters 
regarding educational grants —funds that are pivotal in shaping the future of the 
State's students—the allocated funds remain unreleased. I, therefore, urge that 
immediate measures be taken to release th ese pending educational grants to Tamil 
Nadu, as this matter directly concerns the future of the younger generation.  
Furthermore, it is a matter of deep regret that less than 50% of the tax revenue 
contributed by Tamil Nadu to the Union Go vernment is returned by the Centre to 
support the Tamil Nadu Government's public  welfare schemes. Therefore, with the 
welfare of the people of Tamil Nadu in mind, I request that in the future, the Union 
Government should  fulfill the requests put forth by the Government of Tamil Nadu 
and provide the necessary financial assistance. 
Madam, given India's geographical lands cape, on one side of the country, the 
Ganges River swells, causing massive floodi ng and destruction; on the other side, 
severe drought prevails. Therefore, I requ est that the long-standing demand for the 
Ganges-Cauvery Link Project be execute d entirely at the Union Government's 
expense, thereby helping agriculture flourish across the entire nation. 
The cost of housing construction is risi ng day by day, making it increasingly 
difficult for the poor and underprivileged to  build homes. In Tamil Nadu, under the 
'Kalaignar Kanavu Illam' (Kalaignar  Dream Home) scheme, a subsidy of ₹3.50 lakhs 
is currently provided per house. Theref ore, the Union Government must allocate 
additional funds for housing schemes aimed at  the poor. I, specifically request, that 
this subsidy be increased to a minimum of ₹5 lakhs per house.  Across the country, 
proper building infrastructure must be  established for a ll Anganwadi centers,

Madam, among all the states in India, it is only in Tamil Nadu that free

SHRI YERRAM VENKATA SUBBA REDDY (Andhra Pradesh) :  Madam, Vice-
Chairman, the House is discussing the Appropriation (No. 2) Bill 2026, which reflects 
the Government's expenditure priorities for the financial year. It is important that these 
allocations translate into effective outcom es on the ground, reaching the intended 
beneficiaries. In this context, I woul d like to place before the House certain 
observations and suggestions.  Number on e is education and employment. Outcome 
targets are not just outlay.  The Union Budget 2026-2027 allocates about Rs 1.39 lakh 
crores for education, reflecting an increase of 8 to 9 per cent over the previous year. It 
includes important initiatives such as co ntent creation laboratories in 15,000 
secondary schools and 500 colleges, univers ity-industry-integrated townships, and 
girls' hostels. These are welcome steps and show the Government's intent to 
strengthen the education and skilling system. 
Andhra Pradesh has more than 60 per cen t of its population below the age of 
35. Yet, the ground reality reflects serious gaps in implementation. There are delays in 
fees reimbursement and weakening of student support systems, which directly affect 
access to education for economically weaker sections. 
Madam, number two is coastal economy.  Andhra Pradesh contributes nearly 
60 per cent of India's marine exports, with districts like Eluru and West Godavari alone 
producing about 12.49 lakh tonnes of fish and shrimp, valued at  Rs. 29,211 crores, 
supporting nearly 27,000 farmers.  The Stat e's total exports stand at approximately 
Rs. 1,60,000 crores. Yet, the benefits are not adequately reachin g producers on the 
ground. In this context, there is a need for the Government to establish dedicated 
Coastal Livelihoods and Export Mission with focused support on cold chain 
infrastructure, harbour modernization, and marine processing clusters. 
Number three is fiscal utilization and h eavy borrowing, without development.   
Madam, public expenditure must create as sets and improve lives. However, Andhra 
Pradesh is facing a serious financial situ ation. The State has borrowed around Rs. 
3,18,000 to Rs. 3,30,000 crores in a short span of just 22 months. The House must 
recognize that borrowing without development puts future generations at risk.  There 
is a need for the Government to ensure  st ronger fiscal oversight and link expenditure 
with outcomes. Fourth one is, Erosion of State Finances – Impact of Cesses and 
Surcharges.  Madam, another concern af fecting expenditure outcomes is the 
increasing reliance on cesses and surcharges.   Collections have risen from Rs.4.83 
lakh crore in the financial year 2022 to Rs.5.81 lakh crore in the financial year 2026, 
and are expected to reach Rs.6.3 lakh crore in the financial year 2027.  These 
92
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
revenues are not part of the divisible pool, and, therefore, States like Andhra Pradesh 
do not receive their fair share.   This dir ectly reduces the spending capacity of States, 
even when people are paying higher taxe s. In effect, the burden on citizens 
increases, but the benefits at the State level do not improve.  There is a need to revisit 
the structure so that States have adeq uate resources to implement the schemes 
effectively.  
 Now, I come to health allocation.  Ma dam, allocations for health must ensure 
access to quality care at the grassroot leve l.  Andhra Pradesh had earlier achieved 
strong health outcomes, with a Maternal Mortality Rate of 45  compared to the 
national average of 97, an Infant Mortality Rate of 24 against 28, and an Under-5 
Mortality Rate of 27 compared to 32.  These improvements were driven by sustained 
investment in primary and community healthcare. 
 However, the current situation reflects a decline in service delivery.  There are 
rampant delays in payments under health  schemes, shortage of doctors and non-
functional Primary Health Centres in severa l areas, particularly, in rural and tribal 
areas.  Even basic services such as availa bility of medicines and diagnostic support 
remain inconsistent. 
 Next point is about infrastructure.  Three of the seven new high-speed rail 
corridors pass through Andhra Pradesh.  National highway projects worth Rs.1.4 lakh 
crore are planned for completi on by 2029.  The rail alloca tion for Andhra Pradesh has 
reached a record level.  We welcome this  attention to infrastructure. However, 
infrastructure spending must be accompanied by accountability.  We request that the 
Government publish time-bound project dashboards for all major Union-funded 
infrastructure works, with State-wise empl oyment and expenditure data reported to 
Parliament each Budget Session. ... (Time-bell rings)...  
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIM ATI RAJANI ASHOKRAO PATIL):  Please 
conclude. 
SHRI YERRAM VENKATA SUBBA REDDY :  Just one minute, Madam.  Madam, we 
support the Appropriation Bill and offer these suggestions to strengthen the 
effectiveness of public expenditure.  With these observations, I thank you, Madam. 
THE VICE-CHAIRPERSON (SHRIM ATI RAJANI ASHOKRAO PATIL): Thank you.

The first, of course, is in terms of measures that we have taken in the 
immediate Budget.  Now in the Appropriation Bill, we have made provision for what is 
called the Economic Stabilisation Fund.  This is one of the strongest features in this 
package.  This is an instrument of sove reign foresight.  The proposed Economic 
Stabilization Fund with a total outlay target of one lakh crore rupees is a major policy 
innovation in this year's supplementary demands. 
4.00 P.M. 
[THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. SASMIT PATRA) in the Chair.] 
Its purpose is not abstract. It creates fiscal headroom for the Government to intervene 
when external shocks begin to transmit directly into the domestic economy through 
fuel, fertilizer, freight and other input costs.  
Sir, given the conflict in the West As ian region, through which roughly 20 per 
cent of global oil supply moves, any sust ained disruption obviously has a direct 
102
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
implication on us. India cannot remove itself or remove that risk from the world. But, 
what we can do is to prepare for its co nsequences. The Economic Stabilization Fund 
allows the Government to cushion the effect  of sudden price spikes without resorting 
to emergency borrowing that would breach  Fiscal Deficit targets, allows the 
Government to protect households and farmers, and maintain certain stability in our 
economy. But, look at the progress we ha ve made over the years. There was a 
comment made by fellow parlia mentarian, Dr. John Brittas,  that India's reliance on 
fossil fuels is increasing. Let me tell you about the measures we are taking to increase 
our production of non-fossil fuels, a move  towards green and cl ean energy. In 2021, 
Prime Minister Modi was in Glasgow for the Climate Change Summit when he pledged 
what is called ‘ Panchamrita’ and one of the important points of that ‘ Panchamrita’ 
was that by the year 2030, India's total inst alled electric capacity would be more than 
50 per cent from renewable energy and green energy. And I am happy to say that four 
years before that target, we have alread y surpassed 51.5 per cent of our installed 
electric capacity through green energy toda y. So, renewable energy is meeting a 
major part of our installed electric capacity today. He also made the pledge that we 
will produce 500 GW of  renewable energy by the year 2030. Today, we already have 
265 GW of renewable energy. We are producing renewable energy at the rate of 50 
GW a year and we are well on our way to surpass our target of 500 GW of renewable 
energy by the year 2030.  
Sir, today, India is the third largest prod ucer of solar energy in the world; we 
are the fourth largest producer  of wind energy in the worl d. We have a very enviable 
record there. Not only that, we have recently introduced the SHANTI Bill, on which I 
had also spoken, in which we  were able to open up th at sector to both private 
domestic investments and foreign investment s, with a goal of raising our production 
of clean nuclear energy to 100 GW by 2047. So, we are moving towards a cleaner and 
greener energy mix. Keep in mi nd that India is the initiato r of major international level 
efforts to make the planet greener. The Intern ational Solar Alliance is India's initiative. 
The Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastr ucture is India's initiative. The Global 
Biofuels Alliance, which was initiated duri ng our G20 Presidency in November, 2023, 
was also a major factor in contributing to our ethanol mix. In our petrol, today, we 
have 20 per cent ethanol mix, which is very, very useful in reducing our dependency 
on imported fossil fuels.  
Sir, now, if we look at this particular Appropriation Bill before us, we have 
many measures today that are designed to mitigate the impact of what is happening 
in West Asia by moving, as I said, towards a more modernized and a greener system

So today, I will emphasize my speech over  the entire North-East region.  The 
Union Budget 2026-2027 has allocated sign ificant funds for the development of 
North-Eastern States.  Here are some key allocations.  Number one is the Ministry of 
Development of North-Eastern Region (DoN ER).  One of the headline figures for 
North-Eastern region in this Budget is the enhanced allocation  for the Ministry of 
DoNER.  For 2026-2027, DoNER  has been entrusted with Rs. 6,812.30 crore--up 
from Rs 5,915 crore in the previous year, reflecting a year-on-year growth that signals 
continued federal priority.   Number two is North-East Special Infrastructure 
Development Scheme (NESIDS)-Rs. 2,500 crores dedicated to water supply, 
electrification, urban upgradation, logistics nodes and large-mile  connectivity across 
the region.  Number three is North- East Council’s Programmes-Rs. 825 crores, 
which empower decentralized planning and execution of development priorities at the 
State and local levels.  These funds will be leveraged to reduce isolation, strengthen 
roads and rail federal links and  integrate economic hubs with national markets.  
Number four is   Prime Minister's Development Initiative for the North-East (PM-
DevINE)--Rs. 2,300  crores, a flexible financial wi ndow supporting region-specific 
projects, especially in transport,  community infrastructure and local livelihood 
empowerment.  Number five, ULFA Rehabilitat ion Package--Rs. 500 crores for 
rehabilitation of former ULFA cadres and their families in Assam.   Number six is Urban 
Development. Urban Developmen t has emerged as a priority area in this Budget. A 
dedicated allocation of Rs 5,000 crores ha s been earmarked for the development of 
Tier-II and Tier-III cities, including Guwahati, Dibrugarh, Silchar, Imphal and A gartala. 
This funding will be utilized for public transport systems, waste management and 
sanitation, digital governance platform, affordable  housing and small infrastructure.   
Number seven is Capital Investment Support.  Under the Special Assistance to States 
for Capital Investment, Assam will benefit from  the Centre's expanded envelope of 
approximately Rs. 2 lakh crores, enabling acceleration of  major infrastructure 
projects, urban renewal and logistic development.  Number eight is increase in tax 
106
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
devolution.  Another most consequential measure for the North-East in the Budget is 
increase in tax devolution from 3.128 per cent to 3.258 per cent.  This enhanced share 
strengthens the financial autonomy of North-Eastern States, enabling them  to 
undertake development projects tailored to local needs.   Number nine is Tourism and 
the Cultural Economy.  One of the most transformative provisions of this Budget for 
the North-East is the focus  on tourism as an engine of inclusive development.  The 
Budget outlines a dedicated framework to develop Buddhist circuits across Arunachal 
Pradesh, Assam, Manipur, Mizoram, Sikki m and Tripura. These corridors, grounded 
in both historical significance and cultural heritage, will receive infrastructure support 
for pilgrims and meditation centres, accessible visitor services, digital and interpretive 
cultural trails, linkages with international tourism and tourism markets. By expanding 
tourism infrastructure, the North-East is positioned to attract visitors from South Asia, 
South-East Asia and East Asia, helping lo cal economies reap the benefits of cross-
border mobility, hospitality services and ar tisanal enterprises. Tourism growth, in-
turn, catalyzes jobs in hotels, travel serv ices, transportation, local craft markets and 
environmental conservation, especially , in remote areas where alternative 
employment opportunities are limited. Number ten is human capital, skill and 
education. Budget 2026-2027 underscored the role of human capital in driving 
sustained development. For the North-East, th is translates into investment in skilling 
and vocational training. These programs aim to increase employability in service 
sectors, improve digital literacy, support specialized training for tourism, healthcare 
and creative industries.  
Now I come to education and institut ional support. The Budget includes 
expanded access to quality education, digital learning platforms and institution 
building, especially, in remote and tribal  communities. Next is healthcare access and 
special services. A major healthcare milest one is the upgradation of the National 
Mental Health Institution at Tezpur into a regional apex institute. This will enhance 
specialized mental healthcare services, trai ning and research for the entire North-
East. Next is MSMEs, entrep reneurship and job landscape. MSMEs remain central to 
job creation in the North-East and form the backbone of the North-East’s economic 
activity. This Budget strengthened these sectors by advocating credit access 
expansion, technology adoption support, market linkage programmes and start-up 
facilitation. Number eleven is infrastruc ture and connectivity. The Government has 
approved a special 34-kilometre stretch of underground railway track in the 
strategically vital ‘Chicken Neck’ corridor, strengthening strategic and border 
connectivity, ensuring uninterrupted rail movement, enhanced national security and 
safeguard supply lines to the region in times of natural and geo-political challenges.

Number twelve is agriculture, forestry and rural livelihoods. The North-East’s growth 
narrative cannot be separated from its ecol ogical base and this Budget recognizes 
this through support for environment-aligned agriculture and rural economies. Rural 
development is further supported through in tegrated value chain financing and credit 
linkage for small producers. From st rengthened connectivity and ecological 
agriculture to tourism growth, human capital formation and entrepreneurial 
empowerment, the North-East stands to be nefit from a multi-sectoral push that 
reflects its potential as a growth engine of India's Eastern frontier. These fiscal 
foundations laid in this Budget could well redefine the development trajectory of the 
North-East bridging peripheries to growth  and turning geographical challenges into 
economic opportunities. Sir, with these wo rds, I wholeheartedly support the Bill and 
conclude my speech. Thank you.

Sir, I rise with a deep sense of privile ge as a practising doctor.  I speak not 
merely as a legislator but as someone who has stood at the bedside of patients, seen 
the suffering of the poor when heal thcare was unaffordable and witnessed 
preventable deaths.  In medicine, we say Primum non nocere, meaning  ‘First, do no 
harm’.  In governance too, the first duty of a welfare state is to ensure that its citizens 
do not suffer for want of basic healthcare.   And I am proud to say that under the 
visionary leadership of our Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, the Ministry has not 
merely done ‘no harm’, but it has done extraordinarily good. The transformation of 
India's healthcare landscape ov er the last twelve years is one of the most compelling 
stories of governance reform in independent India's history. 
Sir, budgets are a statement of prioriti es.  In the financial year 2014-15, the 
Health Ministry allocation was approximatel y Rs. 36,180 crore.  Today, in the Union 
Budget 2026–27, it stands at Rs. 1,06,530 crore.  It is a 194 per cent leap.  An 
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
121

 
 
 
additional amount of Rs. 70,000 crore wa s infused into the sector. This year's 
allocation alone is nearly ten per cent higher than the Revised Estimates of 2025-26. 
Two hundred per cent growth in twelve y ears is not incremental reform.  It is a 
paradigm shift that has moved health from a welfare footnote to a strategic national 
priority. 
Sir, as a doctor, I have always believed that the greatest injustice in healthcare 
is not the absence of technology but th e absence of access.  Ayushman Bharat - 
Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yoja na provides Rs. 5 lakh health cover per family per 
year to approximately 55 crore beneficiarie s.  These are 12.37 crore poor families 
which form the bottom 40 per cent of our po pulation. This year's allocation is Rs. 
9,500 crore. What is particularly noteworthy  is the bold expansion to cover six crore 
senior citizens aged 70 and above under the Vaya Vandana Card, irrespective of 
socio-economic status. Nearly half of the 32,000 empanelled hospitals are private.  
Public Private Partnership is now a functioning reality.  
Prevention is the first line of defence.  Under the National Health Mission, 1.81 
lakh Ayushman Arogya Mandirs have been established across the country.  These are 
not mere buildings.  They deliver comprehe nsive primary healthcare - reproductive 
and child health, communicable and non-communicable diseases, rehabilitation and 
palliative care - right at the doorstep of the last citizen.  Over 6.37 crore wellness 
sessions, including yoga and meditation, have been conducted. Schemes like 
Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Pa riyojana and free diagnostics have 
substantially reduced out-of-pocket expenditur e which is the single largest driver of 
healthcare-induced poverty.That is a vicious cycle. When people thought of AIIMS, 
they once thought only of Delh i.  That era is over.  Un der Pradhan Mantri Swasthya 
Suraksha Yojana, 22 new AIIMS have been approved – 18 are already fully functional. 
Fifteen of these were approved after 2014; in the last three years alone, they have 
served over three crore OPD and 15 lakh IPD patients. The PM-ABHIM Mission has 
seen 67.66 per cent surge in allocation. Crucially, Emergency and Trauma Care 
Centres will now be established in every dist rict hospital, ensuring 24×7 golden-hour 
care that will save countless lives.  
Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, cancer is no lo nger a disease of the affluent. The 
Budget expands cancer centres, super-special ty blocks, transplant units and robotic 
surgery facilities. Most significantly, full exemption of Basic Customs Duty on 17 life-
saving cancer drugs will bring targeted ther apies and immunotherapy within reach of 
ordinary families, which was nearly impossibl e before because all these therapies are 
122
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
very expensive and are available only at limited  centres. That is why, it is a great step 
towards healthcare services. Seven additional rare diseases have been included for 
import-duty exemption because rarity must  never mean neglect. In vaccination, the 
inclusion of India's own quadrivalent HP V vaccine in the Universal Immunisation 
Programme for girls aged between 9-14 years is a landmark in preventive oncology. It 
will prevent thousands of cer vical cancer deaths every ye ar. Nationally, Maternal 
Mortality Rate has fallen from 254 to 97, In fant Mortality Rate from 58 to 28, and life 
expectancy has risen to around 70 years. That is a great achievement.  
Respected Sir, no healthcare system is better than the quality of its human 
resources. Medical colleges have increased 111 per cent, to 818; MBBS seats have 
grown 151 per cent, to over 1.28 lakh; post graduate seats have risen 172 per cent. 
New colleges are deliberately being set up in  underserved and aspirational districts. 
Research is equally vital. The allocation to the Department of Health Research is up 
24 per cent; ICMR up nearly 27 per cent. The ambitious Rs. 10,000 crore Bio Pharma 
Shakti initiative over five years will build  domestic biologics and biosimilar ecosystem, 
reduce import dependence and position India as a global biopharma manufacturing 
hub, including a network of 1,000 accredited clinical trial sites.  
Sir, when I look at the journey -- from Rs. 36,000 crore in 2014-15 to over Rs. 1 
l a k h  c r o r e  t o d a y ;  1 . 8 1  l a k h  A y u s h m a n  Arogya Mandirs; 22 new AIIMS; 55 crore 
beneficiaries under PM-JAY; cancer drugs freed from duties; rare-disease patients 
protected; and, Rs. 10,000 crore biopharma revolution -- I see a Government that 
has made an irreversible commitment to the health of every Indian. There is still much 
to do, but the direction is right, the intent  is clear and the investment is real. As a 
doctor who entered politics because polic y shapes health outcomes more than 
individual clinical skill, I can say with conviction that India's health system has turned a 
corner.  
Sir, I began with ‘Primum non nocere’. Let me close with another Latin phrase, 
that is, ‘Salus populi suprema lex esto ’ – the health of the people shall be the 
supreme law. That is the principle that guides  this Ministry. That is the principle that 
must guide this Parliament. Lastly, I support  this Appropriation Bill, 2026. Thank you. 
Jai Hind!

SPECIAL MENTIONS 
Concern over protection and dignity of domestic workers 
DR. K. LAXMAN (Uttar Pradesh):   Sir, I draw the attention of Government to the 
condition of domestic workers -- one of the most vulnerable segments of India’s 
unorganized workforce. Domestic workers perform essential services such as 
cleaning, cooking, caregiving, and childcare,  supporting millions of households and 
enabling the functioning of urban economies. According to the E-Shram portal, about 
2.89 crore belong to the domestic and household workers category. A large 
proportion of them is women and migrants  from economically weaker backgrounds. 
Despite their important role, most domestic workers continue to work without written 
contracts, fixed wages, social security coverage or proper grievance redressal 
mechanisms. The Government has taken im portant steps to support unorganized 
workers through initiatives such as the E-Shram portal and the Code on Social 
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
127

 
 
 
Security, 2020, which aims to extend social security benefits to workers across 
sectors. However, domestic workers face  unique challenges because their work 
takes place inside private homes, making regulation and protection difficult. Many 
remain vulnerable to irregular wages, long working hours, and sudden termination.  
I, therefore, urge the Government to consider recognizing domestic workers as 
‘essential service’ workers and to strengthen their inclusion in welfare programmes 
through better registration, access to so cial security, and effective grievance 
redressal mechanisms.  Such steps will help ensure dignit y, security, and fairness for 
millions of domestic workers as we move toward the vision of Viksit Bharat 2047.

Concern over tariff fixed in Kundankulam Nuclear power plant 3 and 4 
SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu) : Thank you, Vice-Chairman Sir, for allowing me to 
raise an important Special Mention. The subject which I am raising is concern over the 
tariff fixed for Kudankulam Nuclear Power Plant Units 3 and 4.   Sir, the indicative tariff 
of rupees 5.9179 per unit (base), communicated by the Department of Atomic Energy 
for electricity from Kudankulam Nuclear Power Plant Units 3 and 4—that is, 2000 
megawatts—expected to be commissioned during 2026 to 2027, raises serious 
concern. This marks a sharp increase over the existing tariff ra nge of rupees 3 to 
rupees 4.10 per unit from Units 1 and 2 and is significantl y higher than the prevailing 
competitive benchmarks, including renewabl e energy with storage. The Tamil Nadu 
Power Distribution Corporation Limited has highlighted that such a tariff structure, 
combined with the must-run nature of nuc lear power, would impose a substantial 
long-term financial burden on Tamil Nadu  and other southern States. It has, 
therefore, sought a comprehensive review, including re-examination of capital cost, 
financing assumptions, return on equity, depreciation, and the introduction of phase d 
or back-loaded recovery mechanisms. Furth er, the enactment of the SHANTI Act 
2025, enabling private participation in the nu clear sector, raises additional concerns 
regarding tariff escalation. At present, nucle ar tariffs are determined administratively 
without independent regulatory oversight by bodies such as the Central Electricity 
Regulatory Commission or State Electricity Regulatory Commissions.  
[ 23 March, 2026 ]
129

 
 
 
In this context, I urge the Government to immediately review and rationalize the 
tariff for these units, ensure equitable allocation to the home State, and bring nuclear 
power pricing under an independent an d transparent regulatory framework to 
safeguard consumer interest.

Concern over delay in implementation of Delhi Land Pooling Policy and finalization of 
Master Plan Delhi-2041 
SHRI NEERAJ DANGI (Rajasthan) : Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I wish to draw the 
attention of this august House to the prol onged delay in implementing the Delhi Land 
Pooling Policy (LPP) and finalising the Master Plan for Delhi MPD-2041, both of 
which are critical to planne d urban development in the National Capital. The Land 
Pooling Policy was first notified in 2013 as a transformative reform intended to enable 
participatory planned urban expansion throug h voluntary land pooling by landowners. 
However, subsequent modifications introduced in 2018, including the reduction of the 
floor area ratio from 400 to 200 and the ma ndatory consortium formation requirement, 
have created practical difficulties for small and individual landowners. As a result, 
even after more than a decade since the original notification, the policy remains 
largely unimplemented. This prolonged uncer tainty has caused severe hardship to 
farmers, villagers and middle-class investors who had placed their faith in the promise 
of affordable housing and planned development. Many landowners invested their life 
savings through cooperative and welfare societies, expecting that the policy would 
unlock development opportunities. Instead, th eir lands remain locked, infrastructure 
development has stalled and frustation ha s led to an increase in litigation. The 
situation has further aggravated by the dela y in finalizing the Master Plan, Delhi-2041, 
which is essential for enabling sector pla nning and infrastructure roll-out. In the 
absence of this overarching planning framework, implementation of the Land Pooling 
Policy has effectively come to a standstill.  I, therefore, urge the Government to 
consider mission-mode implementation of the Land Pooling Policy with a clear and 
time-bound roadmap, rationalise procedur al bottlenecks affecting small landowners, 
130
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
establish an appropriate institutional mechanism for coordinated execution and 
expedite the final notification of Mast er Plan, Delhi-2041, so that planned 
development, investor’s confidence, and th e aspirations of lakhs of landowners can 
be restored.  Thank you.

Need for a national legal framework to ensure social security and algorithmic 
accountability for platform and Gig workers 
DR. FAUZIA KHAN (Maharashtra): Sir, I seek to draw attention of the Government to 
the precarious lives of India’s 7.7 million gi g and platform workers. This workforce, 
comprising our delivery riders and cab drivers,  is projected to grow to 23.5 million by 
2030. They are the visible pillars of ou r urban economy, yet they remain legally 
invisible. While the Code on Social Security, 2020, formally recognized these workers, 
they still lack an enforceable floor of prot ection. Independent reports indicate that 95 
per cent of these workers have no insura nce and 90 per cent have zero savings for 
emergencies. Most distressingly, workers face arbitrary deactivation where their 
livelihood is terminated overnight by an al gorithm without notice or a hearing. While 
States like Rajasthan and Karnataka have pi oneered welfare boards, a National Act is 
now essential to ensure uniform rights across State boundaries. I request for a 
solution-oriented approach based on three pillars. There is a need to establish a 
Welfare Fund, financed by a 2 per cent tr ansaction levy on aggregators to provide 
accident insurance and maternity benefits; granting workers the right to know, how 
they are scored and prohibiting ID blocking without human review; and, finally, setting 
up quasi-judicial authorities to resolve disp utes in a time-bound manner. Sir, I urge 
the Government to implement a comprehens ive national framework to protect the 
rights and livelihoods of gig workforce.
